# PLAN READING RULES — v1

**Status:** working specification. Rahul dictates the ground reality; Claude writes it here; Claude Code builds against THIS file, not against chat paraphrase.
**Scope:** Indian residential floor plans (CAD PDF, DWG, scan).
**Date:** 10 Aug 2026 · Source drawings: Neelachala / Architrave sheets.

---

## 0. The governing principle

**Read what is drawn with certainty. Ask about what is not. Never guess.**

Every rule below carries a certainty mark:

| Mark | Meaning | Parser behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| **CERTAIN** | The drawing states it unambiguously | Use it. No prompt. |
| **ASK** | The drawing is silent or ambiguous | Use a stated default, flag it, offer the user a correction |
| **ELEVATION** | Cannot be known from a floor plan at all | Flag it; offer elevation upload |
| **CONTRADICTS** | The drawing states something a building cannot be — a DRAFTING ERROR, not a parsing gap | **FLAG and ASK.** Show the user what was read and why it is implausible. **NEVER auto-correct** (deferred until 3D generation is reliable) |

A number the tool cannot justify must never be presented as fact.

**CONTRADICTS — worked example (14 Aug 2026, confirmed by Rahul).** FLOOR PLAN's
lobby reads as a **15.7 ft opening in an external wall on a FIRST FLOOR plan**,
on a floor with no street entrance. In reality that is a wall with a window;
the thin line drawn across the opening is a drafting error. **No reading rule
can reach that truth, because the drawing is wrong.** Catching a drafting error
before site is the tool's highest-value moment for a builder — treat this as a
FEATURE, not an edge case.

---

### 0.2 CONTRADICTS — AN ENCLOSED SPACE CANNOT HAVE AN OPEN SIDE

Ruling 16 Aug 2026 (Rahul). On an upper floor a space has a roof and a floor,
so it MUST be closed. **The only exception is an open balcony, which has
exactly ONE open side.**

Therefore: when the parser finds a space that fails to enclose because an edge
is missing, and that space is not a balcony, **the DRAWING is wrong** — not the
parser. The tool must **FLAG and ASK the user to confirm the missing edge**.

- It must NEVER guess the edge.
- It must NEVER auto-close it.
- The space must not be silently discarded as "outside" either, which is what
  the border test does today.

**Worked case (the first real CONTRADICTS, measured 16 Aug).** FLOOR PLAN's
common area is ONE connected enclosure of **189.9 sqft** (x 27.1-41.2,
y -0.2-18.3) holding the lobby and the staircase, with the lift (23.6) and the
two ducts (5.0 + 2.3) as separately enclosed spaces inside its outline. The
parser finds it correctly and then **DROPS it, because its south edge is not
drawn as a wall**: the free space reaches the raster border and the border test
classifies 189.9 sqft as outside. The south edge IS drawn — as a **2 in double
line on the layer `ar furniture`**, 15.67 ft long (the lobby's printed 15'8"),
below the 3 in wall minimum. Rahul confirms a wall exists there in reality.
**189.9 + 23.6 + 7.3 = 220.8 against the printed 246**, the remainder being
internal wall thickness.

## 0.1 VERIFICATION FIRST

**Where a sheet carries its own AREA STATEMENT, opening schedule, or printed
room dimensions, those are ground truth. Any parser output that contradicts
them is wrong until proven otherwise.**

This check runs **BEFORE** any diagnosis of a suspected parser error. Read the
sheet's own numbers first, compare, and only then reason about what the code
did. A correction proposed without this check is a guess, however plausible
its arithmetic.

**Re-baselining a golden number.** A golden number may be re-baselined **ONLY
when the drawing's own printed data disproves it**, and only with the evidence
and the superseded value recorded in `plans/ground_truth.json`. A golden may
**NEVER** be changed to make new code pass. The old number stays visible in the
file: a corrected observation is part of the record, not a deletion.

**A gap can never be longer than the wall it is a hole in.** When closing the
outer wall ring, a bridge may not exceed the material length of the collinear
wall RUN it sits in. Reject longer spans and log them. The bound is relative to
the wall, not a guessed constant, and not the building's shorter side — on a
95×30 ft strip the balcony faces run along the LENGTH, so a 42 ft opening can
be legitimate while a 96 ft one is the sheet. (Corrected 11 Aug 2026; the
shorter-side version was wrong for exactly that reason.) Where the flanking
walls are shorter than the gap, the space beyond is not a room behind an
opening — it is a balcony or open side, and §10 governs it, not ring bridging.

**UNITS — decide per sheet, never mix.** Indian sheets use feet-inches and
metres, sometimes across one project (GHATIKIA prints `3.43x4.04` in metres;
neelachala FIRST FLOOR prints `13'6" X 10'3"`). Decide by how the number is
written and by plausibility, never by assumption: feet carry `'` and `"`; a
bare decimal under ~7 is almost always metres. A residential room is 2.4–6.0 m
or 8–20 ft. Cross-check the result against the measured scale and require
consistency across the sheet — where the two disagree by more than 2x, our
READING is wrong (the sheet is still ground truth), so keep the previous scale
and flag for review rather than ship an impossible building. One sheet, one
unit: minority-unit readings are misreads and get dropped.

**The building can never be bigger than its land.** Where the plot area is
known — printed on the sheet, or from verified plot dimension text — a computed
footprint above it is a physical impossibility, not a tolerance question. It
**FAILS**; it does not warn. Where the sheet states no plot, the check is
skipped: an unknown plot is not evidence of anything. (This is the check that
disqualified the fixed-kernel footprint: at a 14 ft seal, neelachala FIRST
FLOOR computed 2,563 sqft of building on a 2,455 sqft plot.)

**A quantity derived from the difference between two intermediate masks is
NOT a measurement of the thing it is named after.** Drishti's balcony line is
computed as `foot_ext MINUS foot` — the gap between two internal footprint
masks — so it does not measure balconies at all; it measures how much those two
masks happen to disagree. One eligibility change to the wall ring took it from
42.7 sqft to 0.0 while no balcony on the drawing changed. Any such quantity is
hostage to every upstream change and cannot be ratcheted, because a genuine
improvement upstream destroys it. **Balcony must eventually come from what is
DRAWN** — reading the open edge itself per B-1/B-4 — not from a mask
subtraction. The same test applies to any future figure: name the drawn thing
it measures, or it is not a measurement.

`plans/ground_truth.json` is mode **444 (read-only) on purpose** — the guard
that makes an edit to it a deliberate act rather than an accident. Unlock it
only for an authorised re-baseline, and **restore 444 immediately afterwards**.

**Worked example — FLOOR PLAN.pdf (10 Aug 2026).** The sheet states its own
built-up area: **870 + 835 = 1705 sqft**. The parser reports **1693.3 sqft** —
a **99.3% match**, so the scale on this sheet is right. That single comparison
disproved a proposed scale correction (derived from median door width reading
3.81 ft) which would have shrunk every dimension by ~27% and made the built-up
number **38% wrong**. The sheet's own statement caught in one line what a chain
of geometric inference got backwards.

---

### 0.1.9 GEOMETRY FIRST — the printed total is the CHECK, never the TARGET

Ruling 15 Aug 2026 (Rahul). **Do not chase the printed total.** Architects
count carpet differently — some include bathrooms, some do not — so printed
totals vary by CONVENTION, not only by correctness. The method is:

1. Measure every wall and its direction. Lay the walls correctly.
2. Derive area from that geometry.
3. THEN compare to the printed figure and **explain the difference**.

The gap is information: it tells us what is missing, or which convention the
architect used. A number tuned to hit a printed total has destroyed that
information and can no longer be checked by anything. This is why R-8 is a
ratchet on a DERIVED number and never an input to one.

## 1. What is NOT the building

Before anything else, these must be excluded from geometry, area and 3D.

| Element | How it looks | Rule | Certainty |
|---|---|---|---|
| Plot boundary | Very thick **dash-dot** line enclosing everything | Ignore entirely. Not a wall. Not the envelope. | CERTAIN |
| Setback line | Thin line (often red/coloured) inside the plot boundary | Ignore. Marks the buildable limit, not the building. | CERTAIN |
| Dimension lines | Thin lines with arrowheads/ticks at both ends, carrying a number, sitting OUTSIDE the building | Ignore as geometry. **Read the number** — it is a scale anchor. | CERTAIN |
| Leader lines | Thin line from a tag circle to the element it names | Ignore as geometry. Use only to link tag → element. | CERTAIN |
| Grid/centre lines | Long thin dashed lines crossing the whole sheet | Ignore. | CERTAIN |
| Diagonal hatch **inside the building line** | Repeating parallel diagonal strokes, spanning between blocks or around flats | **FIRST-FLOOR TERRACE — part of the building** (§10.1 covered/uncovered). It is the slab, not open ground, and must NOT be discarded as outside. Worked case: neelachala FIRST FLOOR, where the hatch bridges the 8 ft gap between two blocks — 11 lines span it — and is the evidence they are ONE building | **RESOLVED 15 Aug 2026** (Rahul) |
| Title block, legends, tables, north mark, signatures | Text boxes and tables, usually a corner or edge band | Ignore entirely as geometry. | CERTAIN |

**BOUNDARY HIERARCHY (ruling, 14 Aug 2026).**
1. **Plot boundary** = the **OUTERMOST** line. Usually dash-dot black, but *outermost* is the reliable test and dash-dot only the usual form. Measured signature on FLOOR PLAN: **11 collinear strokes of 6.3–6.7 ft at a constant 3.1 ft gap** — a repeating dash pattern. A railing, by contrast, is a **single unbroken stroke** per line. That gap structure separates them where length and position both failed.
2. **Setback** = an inward OFFSET of the plot boundary — 2 / 3 / 4.5 / 6 m depending on building height.
3. **Building edges** sit inside the setback, though real drawings show projections crossing it.

**Consequence:** the building envelope is the outermost extent of WALLS, never the plot boundary.

---

## 2. Walls

**Signature:** two parallel lines with a gap between them. The gap IS the wall thickness — measure it, never assume it.

| Type | Gap | Rule | Certainty |
|---|---|---|---|
| External wall | wide (**10 inches** in the reference sheets) | Load-bearing envelope | CERTAIN |
| Internal partition | narrow (**5 inches** in the reference sheets) | Room divider | CERTAIN |

**Rules:**

1. **W-1** — A wall is a pair of parallel lines. A single line is not a wall.
2. **W-2** — Measure the gap. Do not hardcode 10"/5". Different projects use 9"/4.5". Cluster the measured gaps on the sheet: the wide cluster = external, the narrow cluster = internal.
3. **W-3** — Sanity band: any measured gap below 3 inches or above 18 inches is not a wall. Reject it.
4. **W-4** — Walls form closed loops around rooms. A gap in the loop is an opening (door/window/arch), not a broken wall.
5. **W-5** — Wall lines may be interrupted by columns, doors and windows. Continuity across those interruptions must be inferred, not required.
6. **W-6** — Wall height is NOT on the floor plan. Default **3.0 m (9.843 ft)** floor-to-floor, which is what the code already uses (`pdf_vector.WALL_HEIGHT_FT`, `scene_builder.WALL_HEIGHT_FT`). The user may override it by entry, or by uploading an elevation. `ASK`

---

## 3. Columns

**Signature varies per sheet.** Some sheets draw columns solid black filled; others as a box with an X across the corners. Within a single sheet the convention is consistent.

**Rules, applied in this order:**

1. **C-1 — Establish the sheet's column signature first.** Find the smallest marker shape that (a) repeats many times and (b) sits on or at wall lines. That shape is this sheet's column. Do not assume a global convention.
2. **C-2 — Columns repeat on a grid.** Regular spacing, typically 10–20 ft, many instances. Something that appears once or twice is not a column.
3. **C-3 — Columns sit on wall lines** — embedded in walls, at wall junctions, at building corners.
4. **C-4 — Size sanity band.**
   - Below **8 inches** → not a column (drawing artefact)
   - Above **48 inches (4 ft)** → not a column in residential
   - Reference bands (Indian RCC residential):
     - IS 456 absolute minimum: 200 mm (8")
     - Practical site minimum: 230 mm (9")
     - Seismic zone III/IV/V (Bhubaneswar = III): min 300 mm (12") shorter side above G+1
     - G+5/6: ~380 × 450 mm (15"×18")
     - G+7: ~450 × 450 mm (18"×18")
     - G+10 to G+25: 600–900 mm, up to ~1200 mm (48") in tall towers
5. **C-5 — Aspect ratio.** Columns are rarely longer than 1:3. A long thin slot is not a column.
6. **C-6 — Columns are a scale anchor, but a weak one.** The existing "column = 12 inches" assumption is unsafe: 9-inch column plans come out ~33% wrong. Only use column width for scale when no dimension text exists, and always flag it.
   - **The 12-inch assumption applies to the column's SHORT side, never the long side.** A column is a rectangle (see C-5): its short side is the dimension the reference bands in C-4 constrain, and the one that stays near the practical minimum across storey counts. The long side varies freely and anchors nothing.
   - **CODE FIX PENDING** — `pdf_vector._scale_ppf` pins `max(r.width, r.height)`, i.e. the LONG side. That is wrong. It must use the short side. On a near-square column the error is small; on a 9"×15" column it scales the whole building by 1.6x.

---

**C-7 A COLUMN'S ORIENTATION FOLLOWS THE DRAWING (Rahul, 18 Aug 2026).** If the
longer side runs along y on the sheet, it runs along y on the ground. No
rotation, no squaring, no normalising, no shared size. *(The flush-inside-the-
wall theory is WITHDRAWN — BRICK WORK LAYOUT.pdf, the GFC brickwork drawing,
disproves it. Do not implement it.)* **Verified 18 Aug: 26 of 26 FLOOR PLAN
columns are built at their own drawn w x d, 6 long-in-x and 20 long-in-y in the
drawing and identically in the GLB.**

## 4. Ducts and shafts

**Signature:** often the same marker as columns (box with X), but distinguished by size, position and repetition.

| Test | Duct | Column |
|---|---|---|
| Repetition | appears once or twice | repeats on a grid |
| Position | beside a toilet, behind the lift, along a corridor, at a flat boundary | on wall junctions, on the structural grid |
| Size | 4–15 sqft of floor area (≈2'×2' to 3'×5') | see C-4 |
| Purpose | plumbing and electrical risers | structure |

**Rules:**

1. **D-1** — Same marker + not on the grid + adjacent to toilet/kitchen/lift/corridor → duct.
2. **D-2** — Size alone CANNOT separate duct from column. The bands overlap (a 25-storey column can be 4 ft; a duct can be 2 ft). Position and grid-repetition decide.
3. **D-3** — A duct is a void. It is enclosed, has no door on the flat side, and is not habitable space.
4. **D-4** — Ducts count in built-up area but never in carpet area.

---

**Worked case — FLOOR PLAN.pdf (re-measured 16 Aug 2026, correcting the same
day's first reading).** Two ducts, stacked vertically, immediately LEFT of the
lift, drawn as plain 4-line rectangles on the layer **`ar wall`**:

| | extent (ft) | size | area |
|---|---|---|---|
| duct-1 | x 25.58-26.58, y 7.89-12.90 | **1.00 x 5.01 ft** | 5.0 sqft |
| duct-2 | x 25.58-26.58, y 4.75-7.06 | **1.00 x 2.31 ft** | 2.3 sqft |
| every column on the sheet | — | **0.83 x 1.33 ft** | 1.1 sqft |

They match a column in NEITHER dimension and sit on a different layer, exactly
as D-6 predicts. **They are NOT in the column set — the column count of 26 is
correct and was never contaminated.** They are not detected as anything either:
at 5.0 and 2.3 sqft both fall under the 18 sqft small-room floor. **The gap is
DETECTION, not separation.** Position matches D-1 exactly: hard against the
lift's west wall, with the toilet above and the lobby below.

**D-7 A DUCT IS A VOID ENCLOSED ON ALL SIDES (Rahul, 16 Aug 2026)** — whether
drawn as a STANDALONE rectangle or CARVED INTO A CORNER using existing wall
runs. The overhang test currently rejects the carved case, and that test asks
a DRAFTING-STYLE question, not a structural one. **Ruling recorded, implementation
DEFERRED**: the only two carved candidates sit on GHATIKIA and neelachala FIRST
FLOOR, which fail everything else, and a guard is not relaxed on the evidence of
sheets that cannot check the result. Revisit when those sheets are otherwise
trustworthy.

**D-6 SIZING RATIONALE (Rahul, 16 Aug 2026).** Duct size is driven by
MAINTENANCE ACCESS: roughly two hands must fit, or the duct opens onto a common
space so it can be reached. That is why a duct is narrow in one direction and
free in the other — it is sized by reach, not by load. A column is sized by
load and is therefore compact in BOTH directions. **A duct may share ONE
dimension with a column; it will not share both.**

**D-5 (§9): a duct is COMMON AREA**, alongside lift, staircase and lobby.

## 5. Lift

**Signature:** a large box, usually with an X across it, with **LIFT** written inside or beside it.

1. **L-1** — Text label is the primary signal. If "LIFT" is written, it is a lift.
2. **L-2** — Position: in the lobby / common area, adjacent or near to the staircase.
3. **L-3** — Size: too large to be a column. Reference sizes seen: 5'×5', 1.63×1.57 m.
4. **L-4** — A lift is an enclosed shaft. It must be built as an enclosed volume in 3D, not left open.
5. **L-5** — Lift is common area. Not carpet. Counts in the building's common area (see §9).

---

**L-6 (Rahul, 16 Aug 2026): build the lift as its own SHAFT** — walls, open
shaft, NO floor. It is a void through the slab, not a room with a floor plate.
*Future (not now): a lift door recessed into the wall for realism — see §11.*

## 6. Staircase

**Signature:** a box containing many closely-spaced parallel lines (the treads), usually split by a partition separating the up-flight from the down-flight, with an **UP** arrow.

1. **S-1** — Many parallel lines + UP arrow → staircase.
2. **S-2** — The horizontal/central partition separating two flights is common but NOT always present. A single-flight staircase has no partition.
3. **S-3** — **Position is decisive.** A staircase is in common area — lobby, corridor, stair-hall. Never inside a bedroom or a flat's private room.
4. **S-4** — The tread lines must not be read as walls. They are floor markings.
5. **S-5** — Staircase is common area. Not carpet.

---

**S-6 (Rahul, 16 Aug 2026): model the ACTUAL STEPS, not a solid block.** A
staircase is treads and risers, and a solid extrusion is visibly wrong in the
walkthrough as well as wrong for area.

## 7. Cupboard / wardrobe

**Signature:** closely-spaced parallel lines, similar to a staircase at first glance.

1. **CB-1** — Inside a private room (bedroom especially), against a wall, no UP arrow, no flight partition → cupboard.
2. **CB-2** — **Position decides.** A cupboard cannot be in a common area; a staircase can. This is the primary separator from a staircase.
3. **CB-3** — A cupboard is furniture, not structure. It must not be read as a wall.
4. **CB-4** — Cupboard footprint stays inside carpet area (it does not subtract).

---

## 8. Openings

### 8.1 Doors

**Signature:** a **gap in the wall** plus a **quarter-circle arc**.

1. **DR-1** — The gap in the wall IS the door opening. Its width is the door width — measure it.
2. **DR-2** — One straight edge of the quarter circle is the hinge side, sitting in the wall gap. The other straight edge is the door leaf when open.
3. **DR-3** — The arc tells you the **swing direction** and **which room it opens into**.
4. **DR-4** — Tagged D, D1, D2, D3, SD with a circle. Typical Indian residential:
   - D1 main entrance **3'6"**
   - D2 bedrooms and kitchen ~3'0"
   - D3 bathrooms and balconies ~2'6"
   - SD sliding door ~7'0"
   - **CODE FIX PENDING** — `tag_openings.DOOR_W_FT["D1"]` is 3.25 (3'3"). It must be 3.5. Where a sheet carries its own door schedule, that schedule overrides this table (TG-5, and §0.1).
5. **DR-5 — Footprint validation.** An "opening" wider than 6 ft or thicker than 1.6 ft is not a door — it is merged wall geometry. Demote it.
6. **DR-6 — 3D representation:** show the leaf **open at the swing angle** for now. (Interim decision; to be revisited.)
7. **DR-7** — Door height is not on the floor plan. Default 7'0" (matches every reference schedule). `ASK`

### 8.2 Windows

**Signature:** within the wall's two thick lines, the wall is interrupted and **2 thin parallel lines** run along the wall. Nested inside the wall thickness.

1. **WN-1** — Window = 2 thin lines nested inside the 2 thick wall lines. (3-line variants exist in some conventions; not yet seen in the reference set — add when encountered.)
2. **WN-2** — The window's length along the wall IS its width. Measure it.
3. **WN-3** — Tagged W, W1, W2, W3 with a circle. This is the SINGLE window table; there is no second one. Typical Indian residential:

   | Tag | Width × height | Sill |
   |---|---|---|
   | W1 main living areas | ~5'0" × 4'0" | **3'0"** |
   | W2 standard bedrooms | ~4'0" × 4'0" | **3'0"** |
   | W3 kitchen/utility | ~3'0" × 3'0" | **3'6"** (above counter datum) |

   - **CODE FIX PENDING** — the code holds TWO conflicting tables: `pdf_vector.LEGEND` (sills 3'0" / 3'0" / 3'6") and `tag_openings.WIN_FT` (sill 2'6" for all three). Which one fires depends on the detection path, so the same tag can produce two different sills. Both must be reconciled to the table above, ideally by deleting one and sharing the other.
4. **WN-4 — Windows are NOT always drawn.** Some sheets (e.g. FLOOR PLAN.pdf) omit them entirely. When absent: **do not invent them.** Report "windows not marked on this drawing" and offer user entry or an elevation. Currently the tool guesses — this must stop. `ASK`
5. **WN-5** — Window height and sill are not on the floor plan. Use the tag defaults above and flag them. `ELEVATION`

### 8.3 Ventilators

**Signature:** drawn like a window, but small.

1. **V-1** — Tagged V or V1.
2. **V-2** — If untagged and the opening is small AND sits in a **toilet** wall → ventilator, not a window. Toilet-context knowledge is required; size alone is insufficient.
3. **V-3** — Typical size ~2'0" × 2'0" (varies), sill high, around 5'0". Never as large as a window.
4. **V-4** — In 3D, place at high level, not at window sill level.

### 8.4 Tag linking

1. **TG-1 — Read the geometry first, then link the tag.** Do not depend on tags to find an element. Tags are labels on what the drawing already shows.
2. **TG-2** — A tag circle may sit outside the wall with a dashed leader pointing at the element. The tag is often NOT on the element.
3. **TG-3** — Link each tag to the nearest matching element along the leader / along that wall.
4. **TG-4** — If two openings sit close together, there will be two tags. Match count to count; if they do not match, flag rather than guess.
5. **TG-5** — Letter + number: **D** = door, **W** = window, **V** = ventilator. The number is a size variant defined in the sheet's opening schedule when one exists — **read that schedule first** if present; it overrides all defaults above.
   - **BUILT** (11 Aug 2026) — `server/opening_schedule.py`, verified on BRICK WORK LAYOUT.pdf ("SCHEDULE OF DOOR & WINDOW", 8 rows, read via OCR). Its W is 6'0" × 4'6" sill 2'6", where our default said 4'0" sill 3'0" — the sheet now wins. No schedule on the sheet ⇒ the defaults above still apply.
   - A row that cannot be read WHOLE is dropped, never half-read: width and height are taken positionally from either side of the `X`, so an unreadable width can never be filled by the height.

---

## 9. Rooms and areas

1. **R-1** — A room is a closed loop of walls. Doorway gaps must be bridged to close it, or the room leaks and disappears.
2. **R-2** — Room name and dimensions are printed inside the room in most sheets ("bed room 11'-5" X 12'-6""). Read them. They are also a scale anchor.
3. **R-3** — Balconies are rooms with one open side. They must still be closed as spaces.
4. **R-4** — Carpet = sum of enclosed room areas inside the flat.
5. **R-5** — Common area (lift, staircase, lobby) is NOT carpet. It is counted separately, per floor, and shown separately.
6. **R-6** — Built-up = carpet + wall material + the flat's share of ducts.
7. **R-7** — Super built-up = carpet × loading factor. Loading applies to CARPET, not built-up.
8. **R-8** — Where a sheet carries its own AREA STATEMENT table, **read it and compare**. A mismatch between parsed and printed areas is the single strongest accuracy signal available. Report both.

---

**R-9 CARPET CONVENTION (Rahul, 17 Aug 2026).** Carpet =
**named rooms + PASSAGE / circulation**. It EXCLUDES balconies, excludes
balcony-type spaces (the utility, §10.1 CE-5), and excludes external and
internal walls.

Consequences, worked on FLOOR PLAN: its printed carpet 1370 = 680 + 690 is
FLATS ONLY, and flat-2's 85 sqft of unnamed remainder (printed carpet 690
against 605.1 of printed room labels) is **circulation**. The utility's 36.1
sqft is NOT carpet, so **carpet stays 1203.5 / 1370 = 87.8%** — the 90.5%
alternative is withdrawn.

## 10. Balconies

1. **B-1** — A balcony is enclosed on 3 sides and open on 1 (the outer face).
2. **B-2** — The open face has either a **railing/grill** or a **parapet wall 3–4 ft high**. The floor plan usually cannot tell you which. `ELEVATION` / `ASK`
   - **RULING (14 Aug 2026).** Railings in these buildings are **2–3 in steel**. The 1.9 in vs 3.0 in difference measured on FLOOR PLAN's two balconies is **sloppy drafting, not different construction** — both are railings.
   - **Resolution path: USER ENTRY.** The tool ASKS which the open face is (railing or parapet, and its height) before building it in 3D, alongside the elevation upload of §11. It must never decide silently.
   - **POSITION IS NOT PART OF THE RULE (ruling, 14 Aug 2026).** A balcony is not always on the outer edge — it can face an internal open space. The measured data agrees independently: the position gate REJECTS flat-1's genuine railing (x −4..−2, outside the trusted wall extent) and PASSES 12 of 14 plot-boundary strokes. Wrong in both directions; dropped.
   - **B-2a RAILING CONTINUITY (ruling, 14 Aug 2026) — replaces per-edge classification.** Follow the thin double line as a connected RUN, tracing in every direction as walls are traced, not as separate pairs. If the run turns and rejoins the building wall at both ends, the balcony has railing on **3 sides**; if it only spans the front, **1 side**. The shape comes from the trace. This dissolves the balcony-end problem: an end is never detected separately, it is part of one run. Measured on FLOOR PLAN: flat-1's balcony is 13.0 ft + 3.8 ft + 3.8 ft = **20.6 ft in one run with 2 turns**, both ends landing on the building wall at x = 0.
   - **Signature (measured, FLOOR PLAN):** a railing is a thin double line, gap 1.9–3.0 in, **9.5–15.2 ft long**, with **no third parallel line** within a wall thickness. A door leaf mispaired against a wall face looks similar on gap but is **~2.5 ft long and always has that third line** (8 of 8 measured). Length and the third-line test separate them; overlap fraction does NOT (1.00 for every class).
3. **B-3** — Default: parapet 3'6" if unknown, flagged, user-correctable.
4. **B-4** — Do not build the open face as a full-height wall.
5. **B-5** — Balcony area is usually counted separately from carpet in Indian practice. Report it separately. `ASK`

---

## 10.1 Covered but not enclosed

**A space can be part of the building without being enclosed by walls.** A
lobby open to the street, a balcony open on its outer face, a porch or portico
— all are roofed, all sit inside the building line, all are counted by the
architect, and none of them close a wall loop. Geometry alone cannot decide
them: **geometry says outside, the architect says inside.**

FLOOR PLAN.pdf is the worked case. The sheet prints `LOBBY 15'8" X 6'2"`
(96.6 sqft) and counts it inside its printed 246 sqft common area, yet the
lobby has **no wall on its south side (a 15.7 ft entrance) and none on its
east**, so the footprint contour correctly traces around it and every
enclosure-based rule discards it.

1. **CE-1** — Such a space IS part of the building for area purposes. It counts
   in built-up, and per its type in carpet (never), common (lobby, porch) or
   the balcony line (B-5).
2. **CE-2** — It is identified by being **inside the building line AND carrying
   a printed label or dimension** — never by wall enclosure. A printed
   `LOBBY 15'8" X 6'2"` is the architect stating that this space exists and how
   big it is (§0.1: printed data is ground truth).
3. **CE-3** — It must NOT be built as an enclosed volume in 3D, and its open
   side must never become a wall (B-4 generalised).
4. **CE-4 — STILL OPEN, but narrowed (14 Aug 2026).** Rahul's ruling: **the lobby is enclosed on all four sides in reality** — CE-4 was partly chasing a DRAFTING ERROR (see CONTRADICTS in §0). **Balconies remain the genuine case** for this category. Do not close CE-4.
5. **CE-4 (original wording) — OPEN QUESTION, awaiting ruling.** *What makes a space "inside the
   building line" when it has no wall on two sides?* Candidates seen but not
   chosen: the slab/roof edge; the column grid; the outermost paired wall runs
   projected across the opening; the printed label's own position. No answer is
   recorded here on purpose — an invented rule would be a guess, and this
   category exists precisely because geometry under-determines it.

---

## 10.2 Dimensions: arrows are DIRECTIONAL and land on EDGES

Ruling 15 Aug 2026 (Rahul). A printed dimension is not just a number — it is a
POINTER, and what it points at is readable geometry.

- **D-1 — pulled perpendicular from the edge it measures.** A vertical
  dimension comes off a horizontal edge, a horizontal dimension off a vertical
  edge. The dimension line is offset from the building; the extension (witness)
  lines run back to the edge.
- **D-2 — the arrowhead ALWAYS lands on a real edge.** If it lands on the INNER
  face of a two-line wall, the dimension is inner-face to inner-face. If on the
  OUTER line, it is the external wall-to-wall dimension. This is universal CAD
  dimensioning, not a per-sheet convention.
- **D-3 — so READ what a dimension measures; never infer it.** Follow the arrows
  to what they touch.
- **D-4 — basis must be stated in every comparison.** Printed dimensions are
  wall-to-wall and typically EXCLUDE projections; measured extents INCLUDE them.
  Worked case: neelachala FIRST FLOOR's bottom block prints 64'-2" (external
  wall to external wall, excluding the bottom-left balcony) and measures 70.8 ft
  including it. **Both are correct. The 6.6 ft is a difference of BASIS, not an
  error.**
- **D-5 — a printed dimension may cover PART of a side.** Never assume one spans
  the whole edge. The same sheet prints 41' on the opposite side, which is a
  PART dimension excluding a 6'7" duct near the lift, with bedroom wall and
  balcony continuing below it.
- **D-6 — a chain along one side should SUM to that side.** Where it does not,
  the gap is real geometry (triangular balcony, duct projection) and is itself
  dimensioned or drawn. If the gap is genuinely unexplained, **ASK** — this is
  the CONTRADICTS/ASK path (§0), never a guess.
- **D-7 — THE FOURTH INSTRUMENT.** Arrow endpoints independently confirm where
  edges are. **If an arrow lands where no wall was detected, the parse is
  wrong.** It works on any sheet that prints dimensions — including BRICK WORK,
  which is blind on R-8, R-2 and the printed-widths check.

**CE-5 (Rahul, 16 Aug 2026) — UTILITY IS A BALCONY-TYPE STRUCTURE, NOT A
ROOM.** FLOOR PLAN's flat-1 utility (printed `UTILITY 4'6" WIDE`) has **two
sides closed** — one coinciding with the kitchen wall, one with the building's
external wall — and **two sides OPEN**, framed by thin gapped lines. It must be
read by the B-2a railing tracer like a balcony, never as an enclosed room.
**Measured 16 Aug: the tracer ALREADY SEES IT** — a 12.33 ft run, 1 turn, both
ends on wall, enclosed depth **4.91 ft**, at x 25.6-33.2, y 19.1-24.1. It is
rejected only because the printed-width list is balcony-only (4'0", 3'6"); the
sheet prints 4'6" for it and `room_dims.printed_widths(toks, "UTI")` already
reads that value. **This run was called a "false positive" in four reports and
is not one.**


**D-8 THE PLATE, THE FLATS AND THE STILT (Rahul, 16 Aug 2026).** A sheet can
state the same floor plate more than once, on different bases, and the
statements CHECK EACH OTHER:

    flats built-up 1705 + common 246 = 1951 = the whole-floor PLATE
    printed STILT FLOOR              = 1900
    difference                       =   51 = balcony + utility PROJECTING
                                              beyond the external wall

The stilt has no projections, so `plate - projections ~= stilt`. Consequences:

- **R-8's basis is the PLATE**, never the flats-only figure. Our built-up is a
  whole-floor number and must be compared with one. Comparing it against 1705
  read **99.8%** while the common area was MISSING from our side — a wrong
  basis and a missing area cancelling. Corrected: **87.2% unconfirmed, 96.6%
  with the §0.2 wall confirmed.** The flats-only row is still reported.
- **THE STILT CROSS-CHECK is a fifth instrument, and it is free** — the number
  is already printed. It needs no area statement of its own: any sheet printing
  a stilt or ground-floor figure can be checked this way.
- **Measured on FLOOR PLAN (16 Aug): our projection beyond the wall is 86.2
  sqft against the printed 51.** Balcony **50.3** (100% outside the ring) +
  utility **35.8** (100% outside). **The balcony alone matches the printed 51
  within 0.7 sqft**; the discrepancy IS the utility, which the sheet's own
  arithmetic does not treat as a projection. Unresolved - reported, not
  attributed.


**D-9 EVERY PRINTED-VS-PARSED COMPARISON MUST DECLARE ITS BASIS.** Three basis
defects surfaced in one week (R-8's flats-only plate, the lobby's wall-inclusive
dimension, the stilt's incomplete projection), so all of them were swept on
17 Aug 2026. State the basis on BOTH sides or the number is not a measurement:

| comparison | printed basis | our basis | agree? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Built-up (plate) | flats 1705 + common 246, incl. projections | whole floor, incl. projections | **YES** (fixed 16 Aug) |
| Built-up (flats only) | the two flats alone | whole floor | **NO — reported, never judged** |
| Carpet | flats only (680 + 690), common excluded | rooms minus common, balcony, utility | **YES** on the common axis; OPEN whether the printed figure includes the UTILITY (36.1 sqft = 2.6 points) |
| Projection (stilt) | plate - stilt = 51, which omits the utility | balcony + utility beyond the wall ring = 86.2 | **NO — and the SHEET is wrong (Rahul, 17 Aug)** |
| Room dims (R-2) | clear inside + ~0.16 ft (2 in) | clear inner face | **YES**, within 0.16 ft on 6 of 8 rooms |
| Balcony width (B-5) | the printed strip width | railing to the wall behind it | **YES**, within 0.13 ft |
| Footprint <= plot | printed plot area | outer face of the wall ring | **YES** |
| Common area | lift + stair + lobby (ducts unstated) | the same types + ducts | measured residual **+5.8 sqft** |

**D-10 A SHEET MAY USE MORE THAN ONE CONVENTION AT ONCE.** FLOOR PLAN's ROOM
dimensions are clear-inside — measured **+0.14 to +0.22 ft (about 2 in) across
6 rooms**, a tight and consistent offset — while its LOBBY dimension of 15'8"
is WALL-INCLUSIVE: 14.09 ft clear + 2 x 0.79 ft of flanking wall = 15.67.
**Never assume one convention per drawing.** Check each comparison against a
case whose basis is already known, and state the basis on both sides (D-9).

## 11. Deferred — to be written

- **FLAT ASSIGNMENT FROM THE PRINTED LABELS.** The sheet prints `FLAT-1` and `FLAT-2` (with their own built-up and carpet figures) and we do not read them: rooms are assigned to flats by an x-coordinate split, which puts flat-1's toilet and utility in the common band. Flat-1's toilet and utility are currently assigned to flat-1 by RAHUL'S RULING and the layout, not by anything we parse. Read the labels, and assign by which flat's door a space opens off
- **Ask the user for NUMBER OF FLOORS (G+n)** so the expected column-size band can be derived — C-4 already carries the G+n bands but nothing supplies n
- **Structural logic for where a column MUST sit** (span rules, load paths) — a deeper enhancement, noted only
- Lift door recessed into the shaft wall (§5 L-6) — realism, after the shaft
- ~~Diagonal hatch marking open terrace on a typical floor plan (§1)~~ — **RESOLVED 15 Aug 2026**: the hatch marks FIRST-FLOOR TERRACE around the flats, INSIDE the building line. Written into §1. Not outside-ground, never discarded as such
- 3-line window variant — awaiting an example
- Chhajja / sunshade projections: currently read as floating wall fragments; must be treated as projections, not walls
- Multi-flat sheets: splitting one sheet into per-flat modules
- Sloped / curved walls
- Mezzanine, loft, double-height
- **Elevation-file upload** — the resolution path for everything a floor plan
  genuinely cannot state: wall height (W-6), window sill and head height
  (WN-5, V-3), and balcony railing vs parapet (B-2, B-3). Until it exists,
  those stay defaults marked `ASK` / `ELEVATION`, never facts.

| 21 Aug 2026 | **THE TERRACE HATCH VERIFIED — and it is a THIRD primitive, not lines and not fills.** Rahul's ruling holds; the recorded evidence for it did not. Measured across the real band **y 67.6-80.7 (13.1 ft)**: **0 wall-segment lines span it, 2 stroked paths span it, and ZERO FILLED PATHS span it** — the hatch is not a fill. ## §13 PLAN REGIONS ON A SHEET (31 Aug 2026, Rahul)

**R-1. A sheet may carry several plan regions.** It is not one sheet, one
building. A user may upload five plans on one sheet or five separate sheets and
both must work. **The splitter's job is to ENUMERATE regions, never to pick one
and discard the rest.**

**R-2. No signal decides what a region IS.** Two tests exist and they disagree in
the real case. **JOIN** — geometry crossing the band between regions: GHATIKIA
has zero ink (separate), neelachala FIRST FLOOR has 19 diagonal terrace strokes
covering it fully (joined). **TITLE** — GHATIKIA prints a title under each plan,
FIRST FLOOR prints one title for both blocks. Rahul's counter-case settles it: **a
master plan with three towers side by side has ONE title and GAPS between the
towers** — one test says one plan, the other says three, and the drawing does not
say which. **Report both signals, decide neither. This is an ASK.**

**R-3. Why the FIRST FLOOR join is real, for the record.** The terrace extends
beyond the tower lines because the design had to hit a parking count without
exceeding the FAR limit set by the adjoining road width under BMC rules. **The
join is structural, not decorative.**

**R-4. The ASK (§0.2).** Enumerate every region and, for each, report its extent,
whether it is joined to a neighbour and by what, and any title text near it. Then
ask the user what each region is — a floor (stilt, basement, first, typical,
terrace) or a block of one floor. **When a title cannot be read, ASK WHAT FLOOR
IT IS.** Habitable space, terrace, stilt and basement are distinguishable by
structure; anything else is asked.

**R-5. GHATIKIA's near-tie ASK is superseded by R-4.** There is no tie-break to
invent — the question was always which plan the user wants, and R-4 asks it
properly.

**R-6. The eventual target is a whole BUILDING**, floors stacked. The region
record must therefore carry enough to support that, not merely to choose one.

# DRAWING THEORY — how a floor plan is actually made

**Purpose.** Every rule we have written so far was derived from a drawing and corrected by the next one. This section works the other way: it states what a floor plan *is*, and what the drafter *did*, so our rules can be checked against the intent rather than against one sheet.

**Status.** Researched 6 Sep 2026 from published architectural convention (AIA CAD Layer Guidelines / US National CAD Standard, ISO 13567, standard drafting practice) and cross-checked against our own ten-sheet corpus. Where the published convention and our corpus disagree, both are recorded — the disagreement is itself the finding.

---

## T-1. THE CUT PLANE — the single fact everything else follows from

A floor plan is **not a view from above**. It is a **horizontal section**, sliced through the building at roughly **4 ft (1.2 m) above the finished floor**, looking down.

Three consequences, and every drawing convention below is one of them:

| Where the element sits | How it is drawn |
|---|---|
| **Cut by the plane** (walls, columns) | Heaviest line, filled or hatched (poché) |
| **Below the plane** (floor, furniture, fixtures, low counters) | Seen in projection, light lines |
| **Above the plane** (chhajja, lofts, beams, upper overhangs) | Omitted, or drawn **dashed** |

**Why this matters to us, immediately:**

- **Walls are poché because they are cut.** Solid fill or hatch is not decoration — it is the section mark. That is why filled shapes ARE walls (confirmed 5 Sep, 30 of 30 duct strips inside a single fill).
- **A window is a gap in the wall with thin lines, not a hole**, because the wall continues below the sill and the plane cuts the glazing. That is why WN-1's two thin lines sit inside the wall thickness.
- **A door is a gap plus an arc** because the plane cuts the opening and the arc is *notational* — a convention, not a projection. The leaf is drawn where it would be at 90°.
- **Chhajja is above the plane.** It should be dashed or omitted, never cut. It is drawn only to show extent.
- **The 4 ft height is why counters, sills and low furniture appear at all** — they are below the cut and therefore seen.

**Rule T-1a.** Line weight is a primary signal, not a secondary one. Heaviest = cut = structure. Our parser currently ignores line weight almost entirely.

**Rule T-1b.** A dashed line inside the building envelope is almost always something **above** the cut plane. It is not a wall and not a room boundary.

---

## T-2. THE DRAWING ORDER — what the drafter did, in sequence

Published professional workflow, consistent across sources:

1. **Set up units, scale and layers** from a firm template
2. **Structural grid** — dash-dot lines with lettered/numbered bubbles
3. **Columns** — placed at grid intersections
4. **Exterior walls / building envelope**
5. **Interior partitions**
6. **Openings** — doors and windows cut into walls, then blocks inserted
7. **Fixtures, cabinetry, stairs, built-in furniture**
8. **Room labels, dimensions, tags**
9. **Title block, schedules, north arrow**

**Rule T-2a — COLUMNS COME BEFORE WALLS.** This is the most useful fact in this section. Columns are placed on the grid *first*; walls are then drawn **to** them. Therefore:

- A column at a wall junction is not coincidence — it is the construction order.
- **Walls terminate at columns.** A wall junction with no column is a partition junction; a wall junction with a small filled/X-marked rectangle is a structural node.
- Free-standing columns exist where walls were never drawn — mid-span in a large room (rare) and throughout a **stilt** (common), which is why a stilt is invisible to a wall-based reader.

**Rule T-2b — THE GRID IS DRAWN AND WE DISCARD IT.** Dash-dot lines with bubbles at the sheet edge are the structural grid. We currently reject dash-dot as "plot boundary". The grid is a *different* dash-dot object, sitting inside the building, ending in bubbles. It states where every column should be.

**Rule T-2c — ANNOTATION IS LAST AND IS SEPARABLE.** Dimensions, labels and the title block are added after the building is complete, on their own layers, usually outside the envelope. This is why annotation is the largest share of unmatched ink (GHATIKIA 60.5%, measured 5 Sep).

---

## T-3. HOW A WALL IS ACTUALLY DRAWN IN CAD

Three distinct construction methods, all producing a "wall", all of which we must read:

| Method | Command | What lands in the PDF |
|---|---|---|
| **Offset** | `LINE` then `OFFSET` | Two separate parallel lines |
| **Multiline** | `MLINE` / `DLINE` | Two parallel lines as one object; **may carry a solid fill** |
| **Polyline + hatch** | `PLINE` then `HATCH` | A closed outline plus a **fill** (solid or pattern) |

**Rule T-3a.** Our parser reads only method 1. Methods 2 and 3 produce a wall whose interior is a **fill**, and we have been reading that fill's outline as a pair and its interior as a room. Confirmed on Typical Floor Plan (94 maroon fills, 5 Sep).

**Rule T-3b.** `MLINE` with Fill enabled produces exactly the "solid poché" case. This is standard, not a quirk of one drafter.

**Rule T-3c.** A hatch pattern inside a wall outline (ANSI31 diagonals, brick, concrete) means **material**, not a room. A repeating family at fixed pitch inside a closed wall outline is poché.

---

## T-4. LAYER NAMING — the published standard and what our corpus does

**The standard (AIA / US NCS):** `A-WALL-FULL-N`
- Discipline (`A` = architectural), Major Group (`WALL`), Minor Group (`FULL`), Status (`N` = new)
- `A-WALL` walls · `A-DOOR` doors · `A-GLAZ` glazing · `A-COLS` columns · `A-FLOR` floor · `A-ANNO-DIMS` dimensions
- **`-PATT` is the poché/hatch minor group** — `A-WALL-PATT` is the wall's *fill*, not the wall

**What our corpus actually carries:**

| Sheet | Wall geometry sits on |
|---|---|
| FLOOR PLAN | `ar wall` |
| Typical / First Floor Plan | `Ceiling` (74%), `0` |
| DHAL-01 / DHAL-02 | `wall`, `RENDERED FURNITURES`, `hatch floor` |
| GHATIKIA, FIRST FLOOR, 19.06 | `0` (85%+) |

**Rule T-4a.** Indian residential CAD is **not NCS-compliant** and will not become so. Layer names must never gate a reader. Confirmed three times independently (Ceiling, columns, DHAL).

**Rule T-4b.** Layer names remain useful as a **hint** — `wall`, `col`, `door` mean what they say when present. They are evidence, never a gate.

**Rule T-4c.** Where a name follows the standard, `-PATT`, `-HATC` or similar suffixes mark **fill of the parent element**, not the element.

---

## T-5. LINE WEIGHT AND COLOUR AS LAYER SYSTEM

In pen-plot workflow, **colour maps to pen weight**. A drafter assigns colour by function and the plotter maps colour → thickness. So on many Indian sheets, **colour is the layer system**, even when layer names are meaningless.

Observed on Typical Floor Plan: walls maroon, room labels and dimensions magenta, toilet tile hatch blue, furniture separate. Layer names carried almost nothing; colour carried everything.

**Rule T-5a.** Colour is a per-sheet grouping signal, discoverable without knowing what any colour means: find the colour that carries the longest straight runs and the most total length — that is the wall colour on that sheet.

**Rule T-5b.** Never hardcode a colour. Blue is toilet tile on one sheet and could be anything on the next. The *grouping* is the signal, not the value.

---

## T-6. WHAT THIS SECTION PREDICTS ABOUT OUR OPEN ISSUES

| Open issue | What theory says |
|---|---|
| Columns invisible on 6 of 10 sheets | Search **wall junctions and the grid**, not the whole sheet. Columns precede walls, so a junction is the place to look. Free-standing columns need the grid. |
| Filled columns not read | Same as filled walls — poché is the section mark. A filled rectangle at a junction, 8-48 in, is a column. |
| Glyphs read as columns | A letter is never at a wall junction and never on a grid intersection. Position, not shape, separates them. |
| Chhajja depth / height | Chhajja is **above the cut plane** — it should be dashed or omitted, and it is never poché. |
| Stilt plans unreadable | A stilt has columns and no walls. A wall-based reader is structurally blind to it. Read the grid and the columns. |
| Windows invented / missed | A window is drawn *within* the wall thickness, and the wall continues below the sill. If no thin lines sit inside a wall, no window is drawn — do not invent one. |
| Annotation as unmatched ink | Annotation is stage 8, on its own layers, mostly outside the envelope. Position separates it from building. |

---

## T-7. WHAT THEORY DOES NOT SOLVE

Stated honestly, so nobody expects more from this section than it gives:

- **It does not tell us the scale.** Still needs printed dimensions, a printed statement, or a user answer.
- **It does not tell us which of two plans on a sheet the user wants.** Still an ASK.
- **It does not tell us the depth of a specific chhajja or balcony.** Those are project design values — read the printed figure, else ask.
- **It does not fix a drafting error.** A wall drawn as an opening is still wrong on the sheet.
- **It does not standardise Indian practice.** Our corpus proves the names are arbitrary; theory tells us what the drafter *meant*, not what they *typed*.

**The value of this section is that it tells us where to look and why — not what we will find.**

---
